Timeline
Action 1.2b: Hire a consultant with expertise in affordable housing to present options for funding affordable housing projects,
identifying affordable housing strategies and opportunities in San Anselmo as the Town budget is developed.
Action 4.1a: Support initiatives that provide tools and funding mechanisms to preserve “at-risk” affordable housing units. Action 4.1b: Assess the feasibility of adopting strategies to preserve affordable housing stock, such as Right of First Refusal (to give
qualified entities priority to purchase a property with an expiring rental subsidy contract), property tax incentives, a one-to-one
replacement ordinance, and housing preservation funds.
Action 4.4b: Assess the feasibility of providing property tax incentives for maintaining existing residences. Action 5.4a: Develop a policy to waive a percentage of project review, permit, and impact fees based on the project’s level of
affordability, up to a maximum of 50 percent for 100 percent affordable housing development projects.
Action 4.4a: Allow for modified development standards where multifamily projects include the preservation of an existing building. Action 4.5a: Evaluate, and if appropriate, amend the Zoning Code and other portions of the Municipal Code to remove potential
constraints for adaptive reuse, such as the review/approval process, design requirements, and parking standards.
Action 4.1c: When housing developments are identified as “at-risk” of converting from affordable to market- rate, work with MHA
to reduce the potential of conversion and preserve or replace units, as needed, through the following strategies:
· Monitor the status of “at risk” units pursuant to Action 4.1d. If property owners indicate the desire to convert the units,
consider providing technical assistance, when possible, to support preservation of the “at risk” units at an affordable price.
· Work with Marin Housing Authority and other agencies and non-profits to seek funding to subsidize the long-term
affordability of the “at risk” units.
To reduce displacement risk and in accordance with California Government Code Section 65583.2(g), require replacement housing
units subject to the requirements of California Government Code Section 65915(c)(3) on sites identified in the sites inventory when
any new development (residential, mixed-use, or nonresidential) occurs on a site that has been occupied by or restricted for the use
of lower-income households at any time during the previous five years. This requirement applies to non-vacant sites and vacant
sites with previous residential uses that have been vacated or demolished.
Action 7.1a: Identify, evaluate, and implement strategies to reduce the number of individuals experiencing homelessness both
Town and Countywide in partnership with the Marin County Health and Human Services, the Marin Homeless Continuum of Care
(CoC), social service providers, and non- profit organizations.
Action 7.2b: Work with local non-profits to create a day where volunteers provide free services such as home repairs and
renovations for low-income elderly and disabled homeowners.
Action 3.1c: Adopt pre-approved infill missing middle housing plans to reduce development costs and streamline the approval of
missing middle housing.
Action 5.1a: Work with Marin County and neighboring jurisdictions to explore the feasibility of establishing a renter match program
for ADU owners and prospective tenants.
Action 5.1b: Work with Marin County and neighboring jurisdictions to explore the feasibility of establishing a home share program. Action 6.2a: Assess the feasibility of waiving all fees associated with ADU permitting when the property owner deed restricts their
ADU to be rented at rates affordable to extremely low- and very low-income households.
Action 5.6a: After the adoption of zoning amendments proposed herein, fund and conduct a Town-owned property study to identify
properties which may be suitable for future redevelopment with housing utilizing the revised standards. Make the findings available
to the public and interested developers via the Town’s website.
Action 5.6b: Using the findings of the study referenced in Action 5.6a, develop a comprehensive program to support reuse of
identified sites for housing purposes.

Planning Division Planning Division Planning Division Planning Division Planning Division Town is partnering with the region to prepare the Affordable Housing Incentives Study. The Study will identify what costs savings to
affordable housing development in San Anselmo can be made and will serve as a roadmap for the Town to implement Municipal Code
Amendments following the Study’s culmination. The Study relies upon engagement with local affordable housing developers and non-
profits to identify feasible options. The Study will examine various options including, but not limited to:
1) Waiving the following local requirements:
a.U lity undergrounding requirements
b.Other oﬀ-site improvement requirements for developments that provide a certain percentage of aﬀordable housing
2) Waiving the following local fees:
a.Permi ng fees associated with undergrounding u li es
b.Waive fees for aﬀordable development projects (consider up to 50% waived fees for 100% aﬀordable developments)
c.Waiving the rezoning fees for property owners’ requests to be rezoned within the Town of San Anselmo’s Aﬀordable Housing
Overlay District
3) Providing grants to cover the cost of undergrounding utilities through the Affording Housing Fees fund.
4) Preserving existing naturally affordable housing through tools such as:
a.Right of First Refusal;
b.Property tax incen ves;
c.One-to-one replacement ordinance;
d.Adap ve reuse ordinance (which could streamline project approvals, reduce parking requirements, and clarify building code
requirements similar to the City of Los Angeles Ordinance); or
e.Housing preserva on fund.
This action is in progress. There are no housing developments in San Anselmo that are currently at risk of converting from affordable to
market-rate housing.
The Town continues to monitor and track units that may be "at risk" of conversion pursuant to Action 4.1d and will work with MHA as
needed to seek funding to subsidize the long-term affordability of "at-risk" units as needed. The Town plans that the local Affordable
Housing Fund, once it contains funds through an inclusionary housing project, could be a funding soource to support the long-term
affordability of units that become "at-risk" in the future.
The Town implements State law as referenced in the language of Action 4.1c.
The Town will continue to discuss this action with the Housing Working Group and identify more ways to help implement it. The Town
works collaboratively with Marin County and regional partners to reduce the rate of homelessness.
The Town is actively working with Habitat for Humanity to establish a Housing Rehab Day in San Anselmo. In early 2025, the Town
identified 5 properties in San Anseelmo in need of home repairs with low-income elderly or disabled homeowners, and Habitat for
Humanity contacted each property owner to encourage their participation in a Housing Rehab Day. All of the owners either declined
this offer or were non-responsive to these efforts. Therefore, the Town is currently partnering with Habitat for Humanity to solicit
applications for home preservation work from San Anselmo residents. As of September 2025, Habitat received 1 application and is
scheduling rehabilitation work with the resident for February 2026.
SB 450 has effectively eliminated Town Design Review. In 2025, the Town will evaluate the impacts of SB 9 and SB 450 on actual
development proposals in San Anselmo to ensure that any pre-approved plans align with real-world needs. At the same time, the Town
is actively implementing a pre-approved ADU plan process. Once the Town has assessed both the development impacts of recent State
laws and refined its approach to pre-approved ADU plans, it will be well-positioned to develop pre-approved infill missing middle
housing plans in a way that is efficient, effective, and responsive to local conditions.
The Town has identified Home Match as the provider for Marin County jurisdictions. This program is costly and would require funding
from the Town’s Affordable Housing Fund (AHF) to be viable. At this time, the Town has not yet collected funds in the AHF because no
development applications have triggered the affordable housing requirements outlined in Title 10, Chapter 21 – Affordable Housing.
Once the Town receives an application that requires a contribution to the AHF and sufficient funds are collected, the Town can move
forward with launching a renter match and home sharing programs for ADU owners and prospective tenants. As of October 2025, the
Town Council recently heard that the cost of program administration by a 3rd party may be less expensive than originally anticipated.
As such, the Town will receive an informational presentation at an upcoming Council meeting to provide information and determine
whether the Town has sufficient resources to pursue said program at this time.
In 2025, the Planning Department considered drafting a policy to waive ADU permitting fees when ADUs are deed restricted for
affordable housing. The Town did not have enough funding in the budget to support this waiver. Therefore, we’ll do this in advance of
this spring’s budgeting process to reassess financial feasibility for implementation in 2026-2027.
This action will be completed, in part, as part of the General Plan Update, which was initiated by the Town Council on November 14,
2023. Tracking implementation of this as part of the GPU in this file: S:\PLANNING\GENERAL PLAN\2023-2024 GP Update\02
Background\Data for Consultant\GPU Key Topics to Address.docx
- This action will be completed following the General Plan Update, which was initiated by the Town Council on November 14, 2023.

- It may also be implemented through the "Affordable Housing Incentives Study" study.
- We could also consider adopting an Adaptive Reuse Ordinance that would streamline approval processes, reduce parking
requirements, and clarify building code requirements (similar to City of Los Angeles Ordinance).

Program Town Lead Consultant? HCD
Timeline
Action 5.9e: Evaluate and update the design review findings in the Zoning Code to be objective in order to remove barriers to
housing development. Remove and replace the findings with objective findings.
Action 9.1i: Work with the County of Marin and other Marin jurisdictions to develop strategies that protect tenants from rapidly
rising rents and displacement. These may include:
· Rent stabilization: Currently, the State imposes rent caps on some residential rental properties (AB 1482) through 2030.
Evaluate a permanent policy and/or expansion to units not covered by AB 1482, as permitted by law.
· Just cause for eviction: AB 1482 also establishes a specific set of reasons that a tenancy can be terminated. These include: 1)
default in rent payment; 2) breach of lease term; 3) nuisance activity or waste; 4) criminal activity; 5) subletting without permission;
6) refusal to provide access; 7) failure to vacate; 8) refusal to sign lease; and 9) unlawful purpose. Evaluate expansion of these
protections or extension if State protections expire.
· Local relocation assistance: Evaluate a countywide relocation assistance program that provides greater relocation assistance
to special needs groups (e.g., seniors, disabled, female-headed households) and reasonable accommodation for persons with
disabilities.
· Right to Purchase: When tenants are being evicted due to condominium conversion or redevelopment, offer first right to
purchase to displaced tenants to purchase the units.
· Right to Return: When tenants are being evicted due to rehabilitation/renovation of the property, offer first right to displaced
tenants to return to the improved property.
· Tenant Bill of Rights: Adopt a tenant’s bill of rights that considers extending protections for subletters and family members
and addresses severe habitability issues and market pressures. This provision could also provide anti-retaliation protection for
tenants that assert their rights and a right to legal representation in the case of evictions.
The Town will take the following actions:
1. Participate in countywide meetings with planning staff from all Marin jurisdictions to review best practices and develop
model ordinances for the tenant protection strategies identified above in 2024. Work with Fair Housing of Northern California and
Legal Aid of Marin to develop strategies and prepare model ordinances.
2. Conduct study sessions with the Planning Commission and Town Council to understand needs and best practices for the
tenant protection strategies identified above in 2025. Invite Fair Housing of Northern California and Legal Aid of Marin to present at
and participate in the study sessions.
3. Prepare ordinances at Town Council direction and bring forward for Planning Commission recommendation and Town Council
Action 9.2a: Require that any development within the Affordable Housing Overlay District be marketed to the disability community
and non-English speakers, including new-immigrant and refugee communities.
Action 9.2b: Require that any development within the Affordable Housing Overlay District prepares and publicizes paper and digital
versions of multi-lingual applications, and that all websites containing application information be accessible.
Action 4.3b: Perform an analysis to compare the Town’s zoning regulations with housing development on the ground to determine
whether amendments are needed to ensure that infill single-family detached housing is consistent in bulk and mass with other
single-family detached housing in the neighborhood to help preserve smaller, naturally affordable housing in the community.
Action 5.3d: Encourage cooperative and joint ventures between owners, developers, and non-profit groups in the provision of
below market rate housing and senior housing. Work with non-profits and property owners to seek opportunities for affordable
housing development on key housing opportunity sites that are close to services, transit, and jobs. Undertake the following actions
to encourage development of multi-family, affordable, and senior housing:
1 - Meet with non-profit housing developers and property owners of housing opportunity sites to identify housing development
opportunities, issues, and needs during 2023.
2 - Select the most viable sites during 2023 and 2024.
3 - Undertake community outreach in coordination with potential developers and property owners during 2023 and 2024.
4 - Complete site planning studies, continued community outreach, and regulatory approvals in coordination with the development
application.
5 - Facilitate development through regulatory incentives, reducing or waiving fees, fast track processing, lot consolidation, and
assistance in development review.
6 - Apply for and/or allocate state and local affordable housing funds to the project.
7 - Require affordable units to be affirmatively marketed to communities of color and protected classes. Utilize publications,
venues, and community groups, such as Canal Alliance, that serve Black and Hispanic/Latinx communities, including outside of
Marin County.

Planning Division We may not even use Design Review anymore (for new housing units) due to SB 9/SB 450. See information on Action 3.1c row for more
details.
On April 9, 2024, the Town Council adopted an Ordinance adding rent stabilization measures and tenant protections to the Municipal
Code. This ordinance was repealed via Measures N and O in the general November 2024 election.
Moving forward, the Town is participating in Rooted in Marin, a community-centered engagement and education process to
understand displacement in our community. The goals of this work include outreach and engagement to all members of the Marin
community, education about anti-displacement measures, and finally a feasibility report that will include best practices.
This will be implemented as part of amendments to the Zoning Code concurrently with General Plan Update adoption. See the list of

Program Action 1.1a: Continue to implement the agreements with the Marin Housing Authority
(MHA), for for-sale units and with local Bay Area and Marin County housing non-profits
and affordable housing developers for for-rent units, to manage deed restricted
affordable housing developments in San Anselmo.
Action 1.1b: Work with MHA, and local Bay Area and Marin County housing non-profits
and affordable housing developers to implement resale and rental regulations for
extremely low, very low-, low- and moderate-income units, and monitor to ensure that
these units remain affordable.
Action 1.1c: Work with non-profits to assist in achieving the Town’s housing goals and
implementing programs. Coordination should occur on an ongoing basis and as special
opportunities arise through Housing Element implementation. Facilitate the participation
of non-profits as an advisory role to assist the Town’s affordable housing consultant (see
Program 1.2) in identifying funding and opportunities for development.
Action 1.2a: Provide a report annually to the Town Council to review progress on Housing
Element programs. Annually prioritize programs for implementation and include funding
in the Town’s budget.
Action 1.2c: Continue ongoing and annual outreach and coordination with non-profit
housing developers and affordable housing advocates to provide advice and comment on
proposed zoning amendments and other program implementation.
Action 2.2c: Regularly reach out to community groups and residents representing a broad
range of socio-economic groups in the community to solicit ideas for housing strategies
when they are discussed at Planning Commission or Town Council meetings.
Action 5.2f: Conduct an analysis of fees collected for recent multifamily housing projects
and compare to the actual time it took for plan check and inspections by staff and outside
consultants to provide expedited review of desired housing developments and waivers or
reductions of development fees where feasible and necessary.
Action 5.9c: Continue to evaluate and improve the streamlined processing system to
facilitate residential development.
Action 7.1c: Work with housing providers to ensure that housing needs for special needs
groups are addressed for seniors, persons with disabilities and developmental disabilities,
single parent-headed households with children, large households, and extremely low-
income households. The Town will seek to meet special housing needs through a
combination of regulatory incentives and zoning standards. Actions 5.7a through 5.7 g,
5.3a, and 6.2c contain additional incentives the Town plans to implement.
Action 7.3a: Continue to support the use of Marin Community Foundation funds for
affordable housing and continue to participate in the Housing Stability program
administered through MHA.
Action 7.4a: Allocate funds, as appropriate, for County and non-profit programs providing
emergency shelter and related support services.
Action 7.4b: Continue to provide resources that support local and regional solutions that
meet the needs of individuals experiencing homelessness. Continue to collaborate with
other agencies to support shelters and programs, such as Housing First and Rapid
Rehousing, and provide housing resources and opportunities for individuals experiencing
homelessness and displacement.
Action 7.5b: Encourage positive relations between neighborhoods and providers of
permanent or temporary emergency shelters. Providers or sponsors of emergency
shelters, transitional housing programs and community care facilities shall be encouraged
to establish outreach programs within their neighborhoods and, when necessary, work
with the Town or a designated agency to resolve disputes.
Action 7.5c: As the opportunity arises, the Town will participate in the funding of multi-
jurisdictional emergency shelter projects such as the Project Homekey project at 1251
South Eliseo Drive and the Homeward Bound of Marin Workforce and veteran Housing
project in Novato.

Notes/Questions
The Town will continue to coordinate with MHA as deed-restricted affordable units come online to
ensure management of said units in San Anselmo. As of April 2025, no new deed-restricted affordable
units have been created in San Anselmo.
The Town will continue to support and work with MHA, which currently manages all deed-restricted
affordable units in San Anselmo, to ensure that the existing deed-restricted affordable units in San
Anselmo are rented and resold in accordance with their deed restrictions.
The Town created a list of non-profit affordable housing developers and affordable housing advocates
(i.e., EAH Housing, BRIDGE, The Pacific Group) and used that list to reach out to the non-profits to
advertise housing development opportunities on October 24, 2024. As projects arise, the Town will
continue to reach out to the organizations that would benefit from feedback from this stakeholder
group.
The Town completes this annually as part of the required State reporting process each spring.
The Town advertised recent changes to the Municipal Code and General Plan to implement the 2023-
2031 Housing Element via email to non-profit affordable housing developers identified through Action
1.1c on October 24, 2024. As additional potential amendments and programs arise, the Town reaches
out to these organizations.
The Town is engaging with a wide variety of community members through the General Plan Update
process. Implementation is ongoing as housing topics arise. For example, in 2024 and 2025, the topic of
co-housing as an innovative housing type was brought up at the Planning Commission, and the Town
Council is considering adoption of regulations in May 2025 that would conditionally allow co-housing in
San Anselmo.
This is done as part of our annual budgeting process at the end of the calendar year. We run reports in
TrakIT to identify staff time spent and modify development fees where feasible.
Implemented and ongoing. In 2023, the Town conducted rezonings, adopted the Objective Design and
Development Standards, created a new administrative path (i.e., Minor Exception) for
redevelopment/upgrades on properties requiring foundation repairs and in flood zone, and adopted new
actions in the Housing Element to streamline residential development. The Town will continue to assess
opportunities to streamline residential development annually.
The Town adopted amendments to the Zoning Code via Town Council Ordinance No. 1179 on August 22,
2023 (Link: https://www.townofsananselmo.org/DocumentCenter/View/30550/Ordinace-No-
1179?bidId=) and Town Council Ordinance No. 1184 on January 23, 2024 (Link:
https://www.townofsananselmo.org/DocumentCenter/View/31090/Ordinance-No-1184?bidId=). These
ordinances provide incentives that support the creation of housing units for people with special needs.
The Town continues to seek additional incentives that support the creation of units for people with
special housing needs as part of the General Plan Update, which was initiated by the Town Council on
November 14, 2023.
The Town supports the Marin Community Foundation and continues to participate in housing stability
programs administered through MHA by virtue of MHA administering the Town’s low income housing
units.
Implemented. The Town allocates funds to the County annually to support homelessness services.
Implemented. The Town allocates funds to the County annually to support homelessness services.
The Town does not currently have permanent or temporary emergency shelters. The Town actively
encourages positive relations between neighbors, requiring community members to meet with adjacent
neighbors early in their land use permit process as demonstrated in the Town's Application Submittal
Checklist posted on the Town's website: https://www.townofsananselmo.org/1180/Apply-Online-and-
Application-Forms. In addition, the Town has recently provided many opportunities for neighbors to
engage and build positive relations as part of the General Plan Update, which was initiated by the Town
Council on November 14, 2023.
The Town allocates funds to the County annually to support local and regional solutions that meet the
needs of individuals experiencing homelessness, such as the development of a multi-jurisdictional
emergency shelter or a veteran housing project.

=== page 230 ===
STAFF REPORT
TOWN OF SAN ANSELMO Item #: 2.6
MEETING DATE: October 28, 2025
AGENDA TITLE: Consultant Contract for the Creek Park Parking Lot
Contraflow Bike Lane Project
FROM: Scott Schneider, Assistant Public Works Director
RECOMMENDATION: That the Town Council authorize the Town Manager to
execute a Professional Services Agreement with Parametrix for the design of the Creek
Park Parking Lot Contraflow Bike Lane Project in an amount not to exceed $158,183,
and find the project categorically exempt from the California Environmental Quality Act
pursuant to 14 Cal. Code Regs. § 15306.
BACKGROUND
It has long been a challenge for cyclists travelling from San Rafael to downtown San
Anselmo to cross Sir Francis Drake Boulevard (SFD) near the Hub. The current
westbound route using Countywide Bike Route 24 is to ride eastbound Greenfield
Avenue to Lincoln Park to Bank Street, cross SFD, ride south on SFD to Tunstead
Avenue, intersect with Bike Route 20, then head north on San Anselmo Avenue
towards Fairfax or south towards Ross.
Eastbound riders from Fairfax are routed from Route 20 on San Anselmo Avenue to
Center Boulevard past the transit station to the Hub using a very short section of Class
2 bike lane, then onto Greenfield Avenue (Route 24). This route is a high stress route
for adult and experienced riders, and some parents may not allow their children to ride
this route.
An alternative route through Creek Park was included in the proposed project list in the
2008 Bicycle Master Plan, and included in updates in 2015 and 2025. This route would
be a low stress route through the Creek Park parking lot, and bring cyclists to the Bridge
Avenue/Center Boulevard intersection (all way stop sign), then down Bridge Avenue to
connect with Route 20 on San Anselmo Avenue.
Item #: 2.6
230

=== page 231 ===
DISCUSSION/ANALYSIS
The Town solicited proposals from three engineering firms, and received proposals
from two firms – Parametrix and Siegfried Engineering. Both firms are highly qualified
and have worked on successful projects for the Town. Staff reviewed both proposals
and selected the Parametrix proposal as the most responsive for this project.
The design work is expected to take four to six months, and includes two public
meetings. The completed design will be brought back to the Town Council for their
approval in spring 2026 for summer 2026 construction if construction funding is
identified.
FISCAL IMPACT
The professional services agreement will be drafted for a not-to-exceed cost of
$158,183. Funding is available from Measure B ($166,142) from the Transportation
Authority of Marin (through the Vehicle License Fee program). Construction funds have
not yet been identified.
CEQA AND CLIMATE ACTION PLAN CONSISTENCY
This project design is exempt from the California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA)
under a Class 6 categorical exemption, because the scope of work consists of data
gathering, analysis and preparation of plans and specifications for a project. No
construction or alteration will be undertaken. (14 Cal. Code Regs. § 15306). When the
plans and specifications are finalized and ready for bidding and construction, a CEQA
analysis will be undertaken at that time.
The project design will propose improvements that will address San Anselmo’s Climate
Action Plan 2030, specifically CAP Measure T-2 (bicycling).
ATTACHMENTS
Attachment 1- Parametrix Scope and Cost Proposal
Item #: 2.6

Parametrix presents the following qualifications and work plan outlining engineering services to support the Town of San
Anselmo (Town) in advancing the Creek Park Bikeway Connection project. Our team will assist the Town in identifying
and recommending a preferred design alternative, preparing design documents, conducting public and stakeholder
outreach, and providing technical support throughout the design phase.
PROPOSED TEAM
Parametrix has assembled the following key team members to lead the engineering and outreach efforts for the Creek
Park Bikeway Connection project. Our team brings the expertise and experience necessary to advance the Town’s goals
and deliver a successful outcome.
Janice Reguyal, PE | Project Manager/Public Outreach
Janice is a licensed civil engineer with 17 years of experience delivering complex transportation and infrastructure
projects throughout California. Her background includes design and coordination on multimodal corridor improvements,
transit facilities, roadway upgrades, and rail infrastructure, with a strong focus on integrating safety and constructability
considerations into design solutions. Janice has extensive experience collaborating with public agencies, utilities, and
community stakeholders to balance design and technical requirements with local priorities. For the Creek Park Bikeway
Connections project, Janice brings a wide range of knowledge in roadway design and geometric refinement that supports
the development of safe, context-sensitive active transportation facilities. Her familiarity with agency standards and
multidisciplinary coordination will help advance the project efficiently from design through implementation.
David Parisi, PE, TE | Principal-in-Charge
David is a professional civil and traffic engineer with 36 years of experience in the planning and design of Complete
Streets throughout California. He leads multimodal circulation and safety design projects from community outreach
through final design and construction. He is a specialist in Vision Zero and in applying appropriate and innovative
pedestrian, bicycle, transit, and vehicular circulation and safety solutions to assist communities in creating safer, more
accessible and sustainable transportation networks. As Principal-in-Charge, David will provide project oversight, serve
as a technical resource, and verify adherence to contractual obligations.
Patrick Echols, PE | QA/QC Manager
Pat is a registered civil engineer with 39 years of experience managing infrastructure improvements for public agencies.
His experience includes roadway safety improvements, Class I and II bicycle lanes, and multi-use pathways. He has
extensive public outreach experience with community groups and bicycle advisory committees. As QA/QC Manager,
Pat will oversee quality management systems and policies to verify compliance, regulatory, and client expectations.
Andres Gonzalez, PE | Project Engineer
Andres is a licensed civil engineer with seven years of experience throughout the Bay Area, specializing in transportation
planning and design. His expertise encompasses all phases of project development, from initial fieldwork and operational
analysis to conceptual planning, engineering analysis, and the preparation of complete plans, specifications, and

PROPOSED SCOPE OF WORK
The following outlines the key components and deliverables included in our proposed scope of work:
TASK 1: PROJECT MANAGEMENT
Parametrix will provide overall project management of the consultant contract with the Town, including project initiation,
project planning, budget and schedule tracking, correspondence, and invoicing. This task will include a project kickoff
meeting to review the project scope, schedule, and deliverables. We will prepare a baseline schedule with milestones
and provide an updated schedule, monthly. Parametrix will also conduct bi-weekly team design meetings with an
Action Item list to document outstanding issues and design elements.
Deliverables:
▪ Project kickoff meeting summary
▪ Project baseline schedule
▪ Monthly progress meeting agendas and minutes
▪ Monthly progress reports and invoicing
▪ Bi-weekly meeting agendas and minutes.
Assumptions: Four-month project duration. Budget assumes nine bi-weekly meetings.
TASK 2: EXISTING CONDITIONS AND ALTERNATIVES ANALYSIS
Existing Conditions and Topographic Survey
Parametrix staff will conduct a site assessment to document existing conditions and gather information necessary
for design, including topographic survey, location of existing utilities, drainage features, potential bioretention areas,
and other existing features or elements to support project needs. UNICO Engineering will perform topographic surveys
of the project area. Land surveying needs for this project include research, aerial imagery, horizontal and vertical
survey control, control points, coordinates, and topographic surveying and mapping. Since the Town has provided
the recent survey covering the north end of the project, the limits of survey to be performed include the access drive
from the intersection of Bank Street and Sir Francis Drake Boulevard to the park vehicle exit on Center Boulevard. The
topographic survey data collected will serve as a basemap for the Creek Park Bikeway Connection conceptual roll plots
and final design drawings.
Alternatives Analysis
A preliminary analysis of the existing width available was conducted and six scenarios were considered. The findings
are included in the Appendix section for reference. Three of the six configurations are feasible as they fit within the
existing available width without proposing a retaining wall.
Town of San Anselmo | Creek Park Bikeway Connection
234

=== page 235 ===
Parametrix will prepare a roll plot using digitized aerial imagery at 1” = 40’ scale showing the proposed design
alternatives, existing conditions, and potential impacts, to present to the Town. The Parametrix team will lead a design
workshop with Town staff to discuss the findings and reach a recommended concept to refine for final design drawings.
Two potential locations for bioretention facilities will be investigated to determine feasibility and added value. If
infiltration potential is found, a geotechnical memorandum may need to be provided to support an infiltration based
design.
Deliverables:
▪ Survey control table and report
▪ Topographic AutoCAD base files
▪ Aerial imagery
▪ Roll plots of alternatives at 1” = 40’ using available digitized aerial imagery
Assumptions: The bicycle facility and drive access may be widened with a retaining curb. The retaining wall design services are not
included in this scope of work.
TASK 3: PREPARE 60% DESIGN PLANS, SPECIFICATIONS, AND ESTIMATE (PS&E)
Parametrix will refine the preferred alternative and prepare 60% final design plans in AutoCAD on 1” =20’ drawings.
The design will evaluate and maintain adequate accommodation of control vehicle turning movements such as public
fire apparatuses. Positive drainage patterns across the drive access and bicycle facility will be maintained and/or
brought to required standard. Bioretention cells will be sized and designed. The plan package will include the following
drawings:
▪ Typical section sheet
▪ Survey control sheet
▪ Plan and profile sheets
▪ Drainage sheets
▪ Stormwater sheets
▪ Utility sheets
▪ Bulb-out and curb ramp improvements sheets
▪ Construction detail sheets
▪ Signing and striping plan sheets
▪ Signing and striping detail sheet
Deliverables: 60% Design plans, specifications, and estimate
Assumptions: Existing drainage patterns to be maintained, pavement improvements include a mill and overlay of the existing access
drive and conforms to existing pavements. In addition, existing trees within the parking areas will be ground down to the necessary
elevation. The plan package will include roughly 18 sheets total.
TASK 4: PREPARE 90% DESIGN PLANS, SPECIFICATIONS, AND ESTIMATE
Based on the Town’s review and comments, Parametrix will update the 60% design plans, specifications and cost estimate.
Deliverables: 90% Design plans, specifications, and estimate
TASK 5: PREPARE 100% DESIGN PLANS, SPECIFICATIONS, AND ESTIMATE
Based on the Town’s review and comments, Parametrix will update the 90% design plans, specifications and cost
estimate. The final PS&E will be submitted by February 2026.
Deliverables: 100% Design plans, specifications, and estimate
TASK 6: PUBLIC OUTREACH
Parametrix will support the Town through a collaborative public outreach process designed to inform, listen, and
refine the preferred alternative. This will include preparation for and participation in a public workshop with Town
representatives and key stakeholders to present project goals, describe the proposed bikeway concept, and answer
community questions. In addition, we anticipate up to two advisory or town hall-style meetings to gather input,
address concerns, and document feedback. All comments received will be evaluated and incorporated into the design
development where appropriate to ensure the selected alternative reflects community priorities, operational needs,
and agency expectations.
Town of San Anselmo | Creek Park Bikeway Connection
